<div id="abstract1" class="ltx_abstract">

###### Abstract

A persistent skill library allows language model agents to reuse
successful strategies across tasks. Maintaining such a library requires
three coupled capabilities. The agent selects a relevant skill, utilizes
it during execution, and distills new skills from experience. Existing
methods optimize these capabilities in isolation or with separate reward
sources, resulting in partial and conflicting evolution. We propose
Skill1, a framework that trains a single policy to co-evolve skill
selection, utilization, and distillation toward a shared task-outcome
objective. The policy generates a query to search the skill library,
re-ranks candidates to select one, solves the task conditioned on it,
and distills a new skill from the trajectory. All learning derives from
a single task-outcome signal. Its low-frequency trend credits selection
and its high-frequency variation credits distillation. Experiments on
ALFWorld and WebShop show that Skill1 outperforms prior skill-based and
reinforcement learning baselines. Training dynamics confirm the
co-evolution of the three capabilities, and ablations show that removing
any credit signal degrades the evolution. Our code is available at
<a href="https://github.com/AlphaLab-USTC/Skill1"
class="ltx_ref ltx_url ltx_font_typewriter"
target="_blank">https://github.com/AlphaLab-USTC/Skill1</a>.

</div>

<div id="S1" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">1 </span>Introduction

<div id="S1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Reinforcement learning (RL) (Sutton and Barto,
<a href="#bib.bib61" class="ltx_ref"
title="Reinforcement learning: an introduction">2018</a>;
Schulman<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref"
title="Proximal policy optimization algorithms">2017</a>;
Shao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref"
title="Deepseekmath: pushing the limits of mathematical reasoning in open language models">2024</a>)
has become an important paradigm for training large language model (LLM)
agents that interact with complex
environments (Guo<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Deepseek-r1: incentivizing reasoning capability in llms via reinforcement learning">2025</a>;
Yang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Qwen2.5 technical report">2024</a>;
Team<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib97" class="ltx_ref"
title="Longcat-flash-thinking-2601 technical report">2026</a>;
Touvron<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib5" class="ltx_ref"
title="Llama: open and efficient foundation language models">2023</a>;
Shridhar<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib41" class="ltx_ref"
title="ALFWorld: aligning text and embodied environments for interactive learning">2021</a>;
Yao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib42" class="ltx_ref"
title="WebShop: towards scalable real-world web interaction with grounded language agents">2022a</a>;
Xi<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib96" class="ltx_ref"
title="AgentGym: evaluating and training large language model-based agents across diverse environments">2025</a>).
Standard RL training treats each task as an isolated episode, where the
strategies that lead to success are absorbed only implicitly into the
policy parameters and cannot be explicitly reused on future tasks. A
natural solution is to augment agents with a persistent skill library
that accumulates reusable strategies from past experience, so that the
agent can draw on previously successful approaches instead of solving
every task from scratch (Wang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib47" class="ltx_ref"
title="Voyager: an open-ended embodied agent with large language models">2023</a>;
Zhao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib39" class="ltx_ref"
title="Expel: llm agents are experiential learners">2024</a>;
Xia<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref"
title="Skillrl: evolving agents via recursive skill-augmented reinforcement learning">2026</a>;
Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref"
title="RetroAgent: from solving to evolving via retrospective dual intrinsic feedback">2026b</a>;
Muhtar<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref"
title="Complementary reinforcement learning">2026</a>;
Lu<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib46" class="ltx_ref"
title="SKILL0: in-context agentic reinforcement learning for skill internalization">2026</a>).
The workflow of these skill-augmented agents can be organized around a
three-stage lifecycle (Jiang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib33" class="ltx_ref"
title="SoK: agentic skills–beyond tool use in llm agents">2026b</a>):
skill selection, where the agent selects a relevant skill from the
library for the current task; skill utilization, where the agent
executes guided by the selected skill; and skill distillation, where the
agent derives new reusable skills from the trajectories.

</div>

<div id="S1.p2" class="ltx_para">

Existing methods have advanced each stage through RL, improving how
agents select skills (Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib48" class="ltx_ref"
title="MemSkill: learning and evolving memory skills for self-evolving agents">2026a</a>;
Wang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib32" class="ltx_ref"
title="SkillOrchestra: learning to route agents via skill transfer">2026</a>;
Li<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib20" class="ltx_ref"
title="Arise: agent reasoning with intrinsic skill evolution in hierarchical reinforcement learning">2026b</a>;
Wu<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib49" class="ltx_ref"
title="Evolver: self-evolving llm agents through an experience-driven lifecycle">2025</a>),
utilize them (Xia<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref"
title="Skillrl: evolving agents via recursive skill-augmented reinforcement learning">2026</a>;
Muhtar<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref"
title="Complementary reinforcement learning">2026</a>;
Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref"
title="RetroAgent: from solving to evolving via retrospective dual intrinsic feedback">2026b</a>;
Li<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib20" class="ltx_ref"
title="Arise: agent reasoning with intrinsic skill evolution in hierarchical reinforcement learning">2026b</a>;
Wang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib45" class="ltx_ref"
title="Reinforcement learning for self-improving agent with skill library">2025c</a>),
and distill reusable
knowledge (Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref"
title="RetroAgent: from solving to evolving via retrospective dual intrinsic feedback">2026b</a>;
Wang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib45" class="ltx_ref"
title="Reinforcement learning for self-improving agent with skill library">2025c</a>;
Muhtar<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref"
title="Complementary reinforcement learning">2026</a>;
Wu<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib49" class="ltx_ref"
title="Evolver: self-evolving llm agents through an experience-driven lifecycle">2025</a>).
Yet two fundamental questions remain open. <span id="S1.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(1) How can an agent evolve all three
capabilities simultaneously?</span> Existing methods apply policy
updates to only a subset of the lifecycle, leaving at least one
capability unoptimized, leading to optimization
bottlenecks (Xia<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref"
title="Skillrl: evolving agents via recursive skill-augmented reinforcement learning">2026</a>;
Muhtar<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref"
title="Complementary reinforcement learning">2026</a>;
Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref"
title="RetroAgent: from solving to evolving via retrospective dual intrinsic feedback">2026b</a>;
Wang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib45" class="ltx_ref"
title="Reinforcement learning for self-improving agent with skill library">2025c</a>).
For example, a policy that has learned to use skills well still
underperforms if it keeps routing to sub-optimal ones.
<span id="S1.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(2) How can the
three capabilities co-evolve toward a shared objective?</span> Prior
designs draw the rewards from different
sources (Li<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib20" class="ltx_ref"
title="Arise: agent reasoning with intrinsic skill evolution in hierarchical reinforcement learning">2026b</a>;
Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref"
title="RetroAgent: from solving to evolving via retrospective dual intrinsic feedback">2026b</a>;
Muhtar<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref"
title="Complementary reinforcement learning">2026</a>). For example, one
capability may receive task-outcome reward while another relies on an
auxiliary signal such as self-assessed quality or heuristic matching
scores. Since the three capabilities jointly determine task success,
optimizing them with inconsistent signals creates conflicting pressures.

</div>

<div id="S1.p3" class="ltx_para">

We present Skill1, a framework that achieves unified evolution of
skill-augmented agents by training a single policy to co-evolve skill
selection, utilization, and distillation. As illustrated in
Figure <a href="#S1.F1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>, given a new task, the policy
first generates a natural-language query to retrieve candidate skills
from the library, and then re-ranks the retrieved candidates to select
the best match. The policy then performs multi-turn interaction with the
environment conditioned on the top-ranked skill. After execution, the
policy distills reusable skills from the experience based on its
rollouts.

</div>

<div id="S1.p4" class="ltx_para">

We achieve co-evolution of all three capabilities through credit
assignment on a single task-outcome signal $`r(\tau)`$. The outcome
directly measures how well the policy solves the current task and serves
as the utilization reward. To credit selection and distillation, we
decompose this signal into its low-frequency trend and high-frequency
variation. The low-frequency trend is defined as the moving average of
outcomes associated with each skill. This term reflects skill utility
and guides the policy toward consistently effective skills. The
high-frequency variation is approximated with the deviation of the
current outcome from the trend. This term captures whether a newly
distilled skill improves upon the library’s current boundary, and
rewards the policy for producing useful skills.

</div>

<figure id="S1.F1" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2605.06130/assets/x1.png" id="S1.F1.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="746"
height="300" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 1: </span> <span
id="S1.F1.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Training paradigms for
skill-augmented agents.</span> (a) The skill-augmented agent loop
consists of selection, utilization, and distillation. (b) Prior methods
delegate some stages to external modules without policy gradients
(<em>e.g.,</em> freezes selection or uses an external teacher for
distillation). Skill1 trains a single policy across all three stages
with a shared task-outcome signal.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S1.p5" class="ltx_para">

We empirically evaluate Skill1 on
ALFWorld (Shridhar<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib41" class="ltx_ref"
title="ALFWorld: aligning text and embodied environments for interactive learning">2021</a>)
and WebShop (Yao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib42" class="ltx_ref"
title="WebShop: towards scalable real-world web interaction with grounded language agents">2022a</a>).
Skill1 achieves 97.5% success rate on ALFWorld, surpassing all other
baseline skill-augmented agents. Training dynamics confirm that
selection precision, utilization success rate, and library quality
improve simultaneously under the shared signal. Ablations show that
removing any single stage’s credit-assignment signal degrades all three
capabilities, providing evidence for their mutual dependence.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">2 </span>Preliminary: LLM Agent with Skill Library

<div id="S2.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Task formulation.

<div id="S2.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

We formulate the skill-augmented agent learning problem as a
POMDP (Lauri<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref"
title="Partially observable markov decision processes in robotics: a survey">2022</a>)
$`\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O},T,\Omega,R,\gamma)`$.
A state $`S=(x,e,\mathcal{B})`$ comprises a task instruction $`x`$ from
dataset $`\mathcal{D}`$, the environment state $`e`$, and a persistent
skill library $`\mathcal{B}=\{s_{1},s_{2},\ldots\}`$. At each turn the
agent selects an action $`a\in\mathcal{A}`$ to send to the environment.
The observation function $`\Omega`$ exposes a partial view
$`o_{t}=(x,\,e_{t},\,z)`$, where $`z`$ is the skill selected from
$`\mathcal{B}`$ via a frozen encoder $`\mathcal{E}`$. The overall
training objective for the workflow can be defined as:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\max_{\theta}\;\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{D},\,\tau\sim\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)}\bigl[r(\tau)\bigr],
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(1)</span> |

where $`\pi_{\theta}`$ is optimized with RL algorithms such as
GRPO (Shao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref"
title="Deepseekmath: pushing the limits of mathematical reasoning in open language models">2024</a>)
(*cf.* Appendix <a href="#A2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix B Algorithm Details ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">B</span></a>).

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2.SS0.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Skills for LLM agents.

<div id="S2.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

A skill $`s\in\mathcal{B}`$ consists of a natural-language strategy
$`s.\text{strat}`$ that describes how to act and a scenario description
$`s.\text{desc}`$ that characterizes when the skill applies. The agent
maintains the skill library $`\mathcal{B}=\{s_{1},s_{2},\ldots\}`$ as it
continuously explores the environment. To reuse a skill, the agent
generates its action conditioned on the skill strategy:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
a_{t}\sim\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x,\,z.\text{strat},\,o_{\leqslant t}).
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(2)</span> |

To interact with a skill library, the agent selects skills from
$`\mathcal{B}`$, utilizes them during execution
(Eq. <a href="#S2.E2" class="ltx_ref"
title="In Skills for LLM agents. ‣ 2 Preliminary: LLM Agent with Skill Library ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>), and distills new skills back
into $`\mathcal{B}`$. In §<a href="#S3" class="ltx_ref"
title="3 Method ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>, we show how to optimize all
three stages jointly through a single policy, deriving every learning
signal from the task outcome $`r(\tau)`$.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">3 </span>Method

<figure id="S3.F2" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2605.06130/assets/x2.png" id="S3.F2.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="830"
height="429" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 2: </span> <span
id="S3.F2.4.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Overview of the Skill1
framework.</span> (a) The policy generates a query and re-ranks
retrieved candidates to select a skill. (b) The policy performs
multi-turn interaction conditioned on the selected skill. (c) The policy
reflects on the trajectory and distills a reusable skill. All learning
signals are derived from the task-outcome <span
class="math inline"><em>r</em>(<em>τ</em>)</span> to achieve
co-evolution of three capabilities.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S3.p1" class="ltx_para">

We introduce Skill1, a framework that trains a single policy
$`\pi_{\theta}`$ to co-evolve skill selection, utilization, and
distillation toward a shared task-outcome objective
(Figure <a href="#S3.F2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 2 ‣ 3 Method ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>). We first describe the
workflow (§<a href="#S3.SS1" class="ltx_ref"
title="3.1 Agent Workflow ‣ 3 Method ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.1</span></a>), then derive all learning
signals from the task outcome $`r(\tau)`$
(§<a href="#S3.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="3.2 Reward Assignment ‣ 3 Method ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.2</span></a>), and finally formulate the
joint optimization objective (§<a href="#S3.SS3" class="ltx_ref"
title="3.3 Joint Optimization ‣ 3 Method ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.3</span></a>).

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.1 </span>Agent Workflow

<div id="S3.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

For each task $`x\sim\mathcal{D}`$, the policy $`\pi_{\theta}`$ performs
three stages in sequence. A complete trajectory takes the form
$`\tau=(q,\,z,\,a_{1},o_{1},\ldots,a_{T},o_{T},\,s_{\text{new}})`$,
where $`q`$ is the selection query, $`z`$ is the selected skill (or
$`\emptyset`$), the action–observation pairs constitute the multi-turn
interaction, and $`s_{\text{new}}`$ is the distilled skill. The
environment returns a terminal reward $`r(\tau)\in\{0,1\}`$. Prompt
templates are in Appendix <a href="#A7" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix G Prompt Templates ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">G</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Skill selection.

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Given a task $`x`$, the policy generates a natural-language query
$`q\sim\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)`$ to search the skill library
$`\mathcal{B}`$. A frozen encoder $`\mathcal{E}`$ retrieves the
top-$`K`$ candidates by semantic similarity:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\mathcal{B}_{K}=\operatorname{top\text{-}K}_{s\in\mathcal{B}}\;\operatorname{sim}\bigl(\mathcal{E}(q),\,\mathcal{E}(s.\text{desc})\bigr).
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(3)</span> |

The policy then re-ranks these candidates by generating a permutation
$`\sigma\sim\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x,\mathcal{B}_{K})`$, and the
top-ranked skill $`z`$ is provided for utilization. Both query
generation and re-ranking are produced by $`\pi_{\theta}`$, so selection
is directly optimizable through the policy gradient.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Skill utilization.

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

The policy interacts with the environment for up to $`T`$ turns
conditioned on the selected skill:
$`\tau\sim\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x,\,z.\text{strat},\,o_{\leqslant t})`$.
For each task, $`G`$ rollouts are sampled independently, each performing
its own selection, utilization, and distillation.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Skill distillation.

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

After each rollout, $`\pi_{\theta}`$ reflects on the trajectory to
produce: (i) a reusable strategy
$`s_{\text{new}}.\text{strat}\sim\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x,\tau)`$
summarizing the approach, and (ii) a scenario description
$`s_{\text{new}}.\text{desc}\sim\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x,\tau)`$
characterizing when the skill applies. A new skill is admitted to
$`\mathcal{B}`$ only when $`r(\tau)=1`$. When the library reaches its
capacity $`|\mathcal{B}|=N_{\text{max}}`$, the skill with the lowest
retirement score $`U(s)\cdot\log\bigl(n(s)\bigr)`$ is removed, where
$`n(s)`$ is the number of times $`s`$ has been selected. This heuristic
retires skills that are both low-utility and infrequently used while
preserving well-tested high-utility skills.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.2 </span>Reward Assignment

<div id="S3.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Co-evolution requires that each capability receives targeted learning
signals from the shared task outcome $`r(\tau)`$. The challenge is that
the three capabilities operate at different temporal scopes: utilization
concerns the current episode, selection concerns which skills are
consistently effective across episodes, and distillation concerns
whether new experience improves upon what the library already covers. We
address this by decomposing $`r(\tau)`$ into its low-frequency trend and
high-frequency variation, assigning credit to each capability without
auxiliary models or additional rollouts.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Crediting utilization.

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

The task outcome directly measures how well the policy executes with the
given skill and serves as the utilization reward:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
R^{\text{util}}_{i}\;=\;r(\tau_{i}).
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(4)</span> |

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Crediting selection.

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Selection improves through two mechanisms. First, the query $`q`$ is
part of the rollout prefix and receives policy gradients through the
utilization objective (Eq. <a href="#S3.E8" class="ltx_ref"
title="In Utilization and query. ‣ 3.3 Joint Optimization ‣ 3 Method ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">8</span></a>). Better queries retrieve
better candidates and lead to higher $`r(\tau)`$, so query quality
co-improves with task performance without a dedicated reward.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p2" class="ltx_para">

Second, re-ranking requires an explicit signal that reflects long-term
skill quality rather than single-episode outcomes. We maintain the trend
of each skill as a per-skill utility score, updated after each rollout
via exponential moving average:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
U(s)\;\leftarrow\;(1-\alpha)\cdot U(s)\;+\;\alpha\cdot r(\tau_{i}),\quad\forall\,s\in\mathcal{B}_{K}.
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(5)</span> |

We update all retrieved candidates rather than only the selected one,
treating co-retrieval as evidence of relevance to the same task
distribution. The trend smooths out per-episode variance and accumulates
each skill’s long-term contribution. We denote the best available
utility as $`\hat{U}_{i}=\max_{s\in\mathcal{B}_{K}^{i}}U(s)`$, which
serves as the library baseline for subsequent reward derivations. The
trend supervises re-ranking by rewarding the policy for producing a
permutation $`\sigma_{i}`$ that agrees with the utility ordering. Here
we use normalized discounted cumulative gain (NDCG) as the rubric:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
R^{\text{rerank}}_{i}\;=\;\mathrm{NDCG}\bigl(\sigma_{i},\;\operatorname{argsort}(-U(\mathcal{B}_{K}^{i}))\bigr).
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(6)</span> |

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Crediting distillation.

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

The ideal distillation signal would measure whether a newly distilled
skill improves future task performance, but that future outcome is
unavailable at training time. We approximate it with the variation of
the current outcome relative to the library’s trend:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
R^{\text{distill}}_{i}\;=\;r(\tau_{i})\;-\;\hat{U}_{i},
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(7)</span> |

where $`\hat{U}_{i}=\max_{s\in\mathcal{B}_{K}^{i}}U(s)`$ is the highest
trend among the retrieved candidates. A positive variation indicates
that the current experience surpasses what the library already covers,
so the distilled skill is worth admitting. A negative variation
discourages redundant distillation.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

</div>
<div id="algx1.l15" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="algx1.l15.2.1.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">15:</span></span><span
id="algx1.l15.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">  </span><span
class="math inline"><em>θ</em> ← <em>θ</em> + ∇<sub><em>θ</em></sub>[𝒥<sup>util</sup> + <em>λ</em><sub>1</sub>𝒥<sup>rerank</sup> + <em>λ</em><sub>2</sub>𝒥<sup>distill</sup>]</span><span
id="algx1.l15.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> </span><span
id="algx1.l15.1" class="ltx_text"
style="font-size:90%;float:right;"><span id="algx1.l15.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_align_left ltx_inline-block"
style="width:173.4pt;"><span class="math inline">⊳</span><span
id="algx1.l15.1.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-fg-color:#4169E1;">
Joint optimization (Sec 3.3)</span></span> </span>
</div>
<div id="algx1.l16" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="algx1.l16.1.1.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">16:</span></span><span
id="algx1.l16.2" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"></span><span
id="algx1.l16.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"
style="font-size:90%;">end</span><span id="algx1.l16.4" class="ltx_text"
style="font-size:90%;"> </span><span id="algx1.l16.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:90%;">for</span>
</div>
</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_float"><span id="alg1.2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Algorithm 1</span> </span> Pseudo Code of
Skill1</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S3.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Each rollout $`\tau_{i}`$ is a concatenation of four generation segments
produced by $`\pi_{\theta}`$: the selection query $`q_{i}`$, the
re-ranking permutation $`\sigma_{i}`$, the action sequence $`a_{1:T}`$,
and the distilled skill $`s_{\text{new},i}`$. We assign each segment its
own reward signal (§<a href="#S3.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="3.2 Reward Assignment ‣ 3 Method ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.2</span></a>) and optimize them jointly
in a single gradient step using
GRPO (Shao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref"
title="Deepseekmath: pushing the limits of mathematical reasoning in open language models">2024</a>)
(*cf.*  Appendix <a href="#A2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix B Algorithm Details ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">B</span></a>), which normalizes rewards
within the $`G`$ rollouts of each task into group-relative advantages.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Utilization and query.

<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

The action tokens $`a_{1:T}`$ are conditioned on $`(x_{i},z_{i})`$ and
optimized by the task outcome $`R^{\text{util}}_{i}=r(\tau_{i})`$. The
query $`q_{i}`$ precedes the actions in the same sequence and receives
gradients through the same objective:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\mathcal{J}^{\text{util}}(\theta)=\mathcal{J}_{\text{GRPO}}\bigl(\theta;\,\{\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{G}\},\{\hat{A}_{1},\ldots,\hat{A}_{G}\}\bigr).
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(8)</span> |

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Re-ranking.

<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

The permutation $`\sigma_{i}`$ is generated conditioned on the task
$`x_{i}`$ and retrieved candidates $`\mathcal{B}_{K}^{i}`$, and
reinforced by the ranking reward $`R^{\text{rerank}}_{i}`$. Since
different rollouts generate different queries, their retrieved candidate
sets $`\mathcal{B}_{K}^{i}`$ differ, thus inner group comparison becomes
meaningless. We thus optimize each permutation independently with a
REINFORCE-style (Williams, <a href="#bib.bib60" class="ltx_ref"
title="Simple statistical gradient-following algorithms for connectionist reinforcement learning">1992</a>)
objective:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\mathcal{J}^{\text{rerank}}(\theta)=\frac{1}{N\cdot G}\sum_{i}R^{\text{rerank}}_{i}\cdot\log\pi_{\theta}(\sigma_{i}\mid x_{i},\mathcal{B}_{K}^{i}).
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(9)</span> |

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Distillation.

<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

The distilled skill tokens
$`(s_{\text{new},i}.\text{strat},\,s_{\text{new},i}.\text{desc})`$ are
generated conditioned on the task $`x_{i}`$ and trajectory $`\tau_{i}`$,
and reinforced by the variation $`R^{\text{distill}}_{i}`$. Advantages
$`\hat{A}_{i}^{\text{distill}}`$ are normalized separately from those of
utilization since the two rewards measure different aspects of same
outcomes:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\mathcal{J}^{\text{distill}}(\theta)=\mathcal{J}_{\text{GRPO}}\bigl(\theta;\,\{s_{\text{new},1},\ldots,s_{\text{new},G}\},\,\{\hat{A}_{1}^{\text{distill}},\ldots,\hat{A}_{G}^{\text{distill}}\}\bigr).
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(10)</span> |

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px4" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Total objective.

<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px4.p1" class="ltx_para">

All terms are combined in a single update:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\mathcal{J}(\theta)=\mathcal{J}^{\text{util}}(\theta)+\lambda_{1}\,\mathcal{J}^{\text{rerank}}(\theta)+\lambda_{2}\,\mathcal{J}^{\text{distill}}(\theta).
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(11)</span> |

The utility score $`U(s)`$ is updated non-parametrically via
Eq. (<a href="#S3.E5" class="ltx_ref"
title="In Crediting selection. ‣ 3.2 Reward Assignment ‣ 3 Method ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a>). The full procedure is
summarized in Algorithm <a href="#alg1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Algorithm 1 ‣ 3.3 Joint Optimization ‣ 3 Method ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>. Training hyperparameter
settings are in Appendix <a href="#A3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix C Implementation Details ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">4 </span>Experiments

<div id="S4.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.1 </span>Experimental Setup

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Environments.

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

We evaluate on ALFWorld (Shridhar<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib41" class="ltx_ref"
title="ALFWorld: aligning text and embodied environments for interactive learning">2021</a>),
a text-based household environment requiring multi-step planning and
object interaction, and WebShop (Yao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib42" class="ltx_ref"
title="WebShop: towards scalable real-world web interaction with grounded language agents">2022a</a>),
an online-shopping simulator where agents search and purchase products
matching user specifications. We report success rate (%) on the test
split for both environments.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Training.

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

For Skill1, the initial policy is
Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct (Yang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Qwen2.5 technical report">2024</a>) and the frozen encoder
$`\mathcal{E}`$ is all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (Reimers and Gurevych,
<a href="#bib.bib44" class="ltx_ref"
title="Sentence-BERT: sentence embeddings using siamese BERT-networks">2019</a>).
We train with GRPO under $`G=16`$ and lr $`=1\times 10^{-6}`$. The skill
library is initialized empty with capacity
$`|\mathcal{B}|\leqslant 5000`$. The training data uses the train split
of the corresponding environments. Full hyperparameters are in
Appendix <a href="#A3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix C Implementation Details ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Baselines.

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

We compare three categories of methods in
Table <a href="#S4.T1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 1 ‣ 4.2 Main Results ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>: (1) training-free agents such
as ReAct (Yao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib37" class="ltx_ref"
title="React: synergizing reasoning and acting in language models">2022b</a>),
Reflexion (Shinn<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib38" class="ltx_ref"
title="Reflexion: language agents with verbal reinforcement learning">2023</a>),
Mem0 (Chhikara<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref"
title="Mem0: building production-ready ai agents with scalable long-term memory">2025</a>),
and ExpeL (Zhao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib39" class="ltx_ref"
title="Expel: llm agents are experiential learners">2024</a>); (2)
RL-trained methods without skills such as
PPO (Schulman<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref"
title="Proximal policy optimization algorithms">2017</a>),
RLOO (Ahmadian<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref"
title="Back to basics: revisiting reinforce-style optimization for learning from human feedback in llms">2024</a>),
GRPO (Shao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref"
title="Deepseekmath: pushing the limits of mathematical reasoning in open language models">2024</a>),
and GiGPO (Feng<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib14" class="ltx_ref"
title="Group-in-group policy optimization for llm agent training">2025</a>);
and (3) RL-trained methods with skills such as
EvolveR (Wu<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib49" class="ltx_ref"
title="Evolver: self-evolving llm agents through an experience-driven lifecycle">2025</a>),
Mem0 and SimpleMem (Liu<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib51" class="ltx_ref"
title="SimpleMem: efficient lifelong memory for llm agents">2026a</a>)
optimized with GRPO, SkillRL (Xia<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref"
title="Skillrl: evolving agents via recursive skill-augmented reinforcement learning">2026</a>),
and RetroAgent (Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref"
title="RetroAgent: from solving to evolving via retrospective dual intrinsic feedback">2026b</a>).
All baselines use the same base model Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct for fair
comparison.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 1: </span> Main
results on ALFWorld and WebShop (Success Rate, %). <span id="S4.T1.15.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Bold</span> denotes best results;
<sub><span id="S4.T1.16.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic"
style="font-size:78%;--ltx-fg-color:#009E96;">↑</span></sub> indicates
improvement over the previous best. “Avg.” stands for average success
rate and “Succ.” stands for success rate.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Table <a href="#S4.T1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 1 ‣ 4.2 Main Results ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a> presents the main results. We
reproduce RetroAgent with the official implementation and borrow other
baseline results from prior
research (Feng<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib14" class="ltx_ref"
title="Group-in-group policy optimization for llm agent training">2025</a>;
Xia<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref"
title="Skillrl: evolving agents via recursive skill-augmented reinforcement learning">2026</a>;
Jiang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib15" class="ltx_ref"
title="Meta-rl induces exploration in language agents">2025a</a>).
Skill1 results are averaged across three runs, and we report statistical
analysis in Appendix <a href="#A4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix D Statistical Analysis ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">D</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Skill1 achieves
the highest overall performance.</span> On ALFWorld, Skill1 reaches
97.5% average success rate, surpassing the previous best RetroAgent by
2.6 points and ranking first on five out of six task types. The
exception is Clean, where Skill1 falls slightly below strongest baseline
RetroAgent, yet this gap is not statistically significant according to
Appendix <a href="#A4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix D Statistical Analysis ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">D</span></a>. On WebShop, Skill1 also
demonstrates the best performance across all methods.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">An explicit
skill library complements parameter-only RL.</span> GiGPO, the strongest
RL-only method, absorbs strategies implicitly into parameters and cannot
explicitly reuse them across tasks. Skill1 surpasses it by 6.7 points,
with the largest gains on Look and Pick2 where composing multiple
sub-procedures benefits most from reusable skills.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.p4" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS2.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Unified
optimization outperforms methods that leave part of the lifecycle
unoptimized.</span> RetroAgent optimizes utilization and distillation
with separate intrinsic rewards but provides no gradient signal for
selection. SkillRL freezes its selection mechanism after cold-start SFT.
Skill1 optimizes all three stages jointly through a single task-outcome
signal. The comparison reveals a clear trend that agent performance
increases with the degree of co-evolution.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.3 </span>Analysis

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">4.3.1 </span>Ablation Study

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

We remove workflow components and zero out auxiliary objective weights
to isolate each design choice. All variants share the same base model
and training budget. Results are reported in
Table <a href="#S4.T2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 2 ‣ 4.3.1 Ablation Study ‣ 4.3 Analysis ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>.

</div>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 2: </span>
Ablation study on ALFWorld (Success Rate %). Upper block ablates
workflow components; lower block ablates training
objectives.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS3.SSS1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">The skill
library is the foundation, and distillation makes it effective.</span>
Removing the library entirely causes the largest drop, from 97.5% to
80.9%, with Heat and Pick2 losing over 28 points each. These task types
require composing multi-step sub-procedures that benefit most from
reusable skills. Removing distillation while keeping the library still
reduces performance by 5.1 points. Without distillation the library
stores raw trajectories rather than condensed strategies, making
selection noisier and reuse less effective.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS1.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS3.SSS1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Selection
loss propagates to downstream stages.</span> Without selection the
average drops by 5.7 points, concentrated on Heat and Pick2 where
routing to the correct multi-step skill matters most. Notably, this
degradation occurs even though the utilization reward remains intact,
showing that poor skill routing bottlenecks the entire pipeline
regardless of the policy’s solving ability.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS1.p4" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS3.SSS1.p4.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">The two
auxiliary objectives are complementary.</span> Setting
$`\lambda_{1}{=}0`$ or $`\lambda_{2}{=}0`$ individually reduces
performance by 3.5 and 2.6 points respectively. Removing both yields a
sharper decline to 90.2%, worse than removing each stage individually.
This gap shows that the signals benefit utilization beyond their direct
targets, confirming that both signals are necessary to sustain full
co-evolution.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">4.3.2 </span>Co-evolution Dynamics

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Figure <a href="#S4.F3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 3 ‣ 4.3.2 Co-evolution Dynamics ‣ 4.3 Analysis ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a> tracks three capability
metrics across training: (1) selection precision, the average skill
utility scores $`U(s)`$; (2) task-outcome reward $`r(\tau)`$ for
utilization; and (3) distillation positive rate, the fraction of new
rollouts exceeding the average of retrieved ones $`\hat{U}_{i}`$. We
compare the full system against ablations that progressively remove
credit-assignment signals.

</div>

<figure id="S4.F3" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2605.06130/assets/x3.png" id="S4.F3.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="830"
height="249" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 3: </span>
Training dynamics of the three capability metrics. Full Skill1 achieves
fast and unified convergence across all stages. Removing selection
signal (green) or both selection and distillation signals (orange) slows
convergence of all capabilities.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">The three
capabilities exhibit mutual reinforcement under unified training.</span>
Selection precision converges first, reaching 0.95 by step 20. The
resulting high-quality skill supply then accelerates the other two
stages, with both utilization and distillation reaching 0.8 by step 60.
This sequential acceleration shows that improvements in one stage
propagate forward through the lifecycle.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Ablating
any credit-assignment signal slows all three capabilities.</span>
Removing the selection signal reduces selection precision as expected,
but also drags down utilization and distillation because the policy
routes to sub-optimal skills more frequently. Further removing
distillation causes utilization scores to drop, even though it still
receives its own direct reward. This suggests that each signal
contributes to the overall growing trend, which is a direct evidence of
co-evolution.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS3" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">4.3.3 </span>Selection and Distillation Quality

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

The previous section shows that capability metrics rise together. Here
we examine the qualitative nature of that improvement: does the policy
actually learn to select more relevant skills and distill higher-quality
ones?

</div>

<figure id="S4.F5" class="ltx_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_2">
<figure id="S4.F5.1"
class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_minipage ltx_align_center ltx_align_top"
style="width:179.4pt;">
<img src="/html/2605.06130/assets/x4.png" id="S4.F5.1.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="830"
height="546" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 4: </span>
Task-skill similarity at three training checkpoints. The trend signal
drives continuous improvement in selection quality.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_2">
<figure id="S4.F5.4"
class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_minipage ltx_align_center ltx_align_top"
style="width:151.8pt;">
<img src="/html/2605.06130/assets/x5.png" id="S4.F5.2.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="830"
height="585" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 5: </span>
Top-skill utility (<span class="math inline"><em>Û</em></span>) during
training. The variation signal drives the policy to distill increasingly
effective skills.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>
</div>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS3.SSS3.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">The policy
learns to generate increasingly precise selection queries.</span>
Figure <a href="#S4.F5" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 5 ‣ 4.3.3 Selection and Distillation Quality ‣ 4.3 Analysis ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a> measures task-skill similarity
at three checkpoints. Full Skill1 improves from 0.51 to 0.60 across
training because the trend signal rewards queries that retrieve
historically high-utility skills, gradually sharpening the policy’s
ability to describe what it needs. Removing the selection signal slows
this learning, and without learned selection entirely, similarity stays
almost flat at the lowest level.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS3.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS3.SSS3.p3.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">The library
ceiling rises as the policy learns to distill better skills.</span>
Figure <a href="#S4.F5" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 5 ‣ 4.3.3 Selection and Distillation Quality ‣ 4.3 Analysis ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a> tracks $`\hat{U}`$, the
utility of the top-ranked skill per task. A rising $`\hat{U}`$ means
increasingly effective skills are entering the library, not merely more
skills. Full Skill1 reaches 0.91 by step 85 while both ablations lag by
approximately 0.10. The variation signal creates this pressure:
producing a skill similar to existing ones yields little reward, so the
policy must discover genuinely better strategies to obtain positive
gradient.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS4" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">4.3.4 </span>Skill Library Diversity

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS4.p1" class="ltx_para">

We examine whether the library is utilized as a diverse collective asset
or collapses to a few dominant entries.
Figure <a href="#S4.F6" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 6 ‣ 4.3.4 Skill Library Diversity ‣ 4.3 Analysis ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a> visualizes the converged
libraries with and without credit-assignment signals.

</div>

<figure id="S4.F6" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2605.06130/assets/x6.png" id="S4.F6.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="830"
height="330" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 6: </span> T-SNE
visualization of the skill libraries after convergence, with and without
RL-trained selection and distillation. The top-10 percent most
frequently used skills are highlighted. Skill1 activates nearly twice as
many high-frequency skills, and these skills span a broader strategy
space.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS4.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS3.SSS4.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Co-evolution activates a broader set of
skills.</span> Skill1 uses a broader set of skills. As observed in
Figure <a href="#S4.F6" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 6 ‣ 4.3.4 Skill Library Diversity ‣ 4.3 Analysis ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a>, the skill usage count
distributes more uniformly in the left panel. Without evolving signals
(*i.e.,* Skill1 w/o Select. and Distill.), the skill usage count
distribution sharpens, where only a small number of popular skills are
intensively utilized.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS4.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS3.SSS4.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Frequently
used skills cover diverse strategies.</span> We also observe that the
active skills in Skill1 span a much broader region of the strategy
space. On the contrary, the popular skills (red and purple ones) on the
right subfigure huddle together with only limited coverage. In the
design of our method, producing a under-performing skill similar to
existing ones yields negative reward, so the policy is pressured to
cover underserved scenarios rather than duplicating successful ones.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS5" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">4.3.5 </span>Computational Overhead

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS5.p1" class="ltx_para">

We compare wall-clock time and library size for Skill1, SkillRL, and two
ablations under identical hardware of 8 H800 80GB GPUs.

</div>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 3: </span>
Computational cost on ALFWorld training. We report wall-clock time per
step (seconds) and library size (number of skills) at three
checkpoints.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS5.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS3.SSS5.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Skill1 adds
moderate overhead over baseline methods.</span> GRPO without a library
runs at approximately 290s per step. SkillRL maintains near-constant
cost because its library grows minimally from 60 to 83 skills, but this
static library limits final performance to 89.9% compared to 97.5% for
Skill1. Skill1 operates at 387 to 494s, roughly 1.3 to 1.7 times as slow
as GRPO, with the increase stemming from the growing library context.
The selection step itself adds negligible overhead as query generation
and re-ranking operate on short sequences compared to multi-turn
interactions against the environment.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS5.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS3.SSS5.p3.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Distillation controls both library
quality and computational cost.</span> Without distillation, raw
trajectories enter the library directly, growing it at 2.4 times the
rate of Skill1. The larger library lengthens the selection context,
making the variant without distillation 69% slower by step 60 and
saturating the 5,000-skill cap far earlier. Distillation compresses
experience into concise skills, improving library quality while keeping
computational cost in check.

</div>

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">5 </span>Conclusion and Limitations

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Conclusion.

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

We present Skill1, a framework that trains a single policy to co-evolve
skill selection, utilization, and distillation toward a shared
task-outcome objective. By decomposing this signal into its
low-frequency trend and high-frequency variation, Skill1 derives
per-capability credit assignment without auxiliary rewards. Experiments
on ALFWorld and WebShop show consistent gains over prior skill-based and
RL baselines, and ablations confirm that the three capabilities evolve
in a coupled manner. A natural next step is to extend this lifecycle to
hierarchical or multi-agent settings, where skill sharing and conflict
resolution introduce new challenges for unified credit assignment.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Limitations.

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

While Skill1 achieves strong performance, several limitations remain.

- <span id="S5.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S5.I1.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Environment
  coverage.</span> Our evaluation is limited to two representative
  text-based agent environments. Whether the co-evolution framework
  generalizes to more environments (*e.g.,* deep search environments) or
  those with visual observations remains unexplored.

  </div>
- <span id="S5.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S5.I1.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Scalability
  of the skill library.</span> The library capacity in this work is
  capped at 5,000 entries. As the diversity of tasks grows, the
  fixed-size library may become a bottleneck, and more sophisticated
  eviction or hierarchical organization strategies may be required.

  </div>

</div>

<div class="ltx_pagination ltx_role_newpage">

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A1" class="section ltx_appendix">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix A </span>Related Work

<div id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Reinforcement Learning for LLM Agents.

<div id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Core algorithmic advances include
GRPO \[Shao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref"
title="Deepseekmath: pushing the limits of mathematical reasoning in open language models">2024</a>\],
anchor-state grouping \[Feng<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib14" class="ltx_ref"
title="Group-in-group policy optimization for llm agent training">2025</a>\],
and dynamic sampling with asymmetric
clipping \[Yu<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib16" class="ltx_ref"
title="Dapo: an open-source llm reinforcement learning system at scale">2025</a>\].
Multi-turn RL methods address long-horizon challenges through
hierarchical value functions \[Zhou<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib62" class="ltx_ref"
title="ArCHer: training language model agents via hierarchical multi-turn rl">2024</a>\],
leave-one-out advantage
estimation \[Chen<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib86" class="ltx_ref"
title="Reinforcement learning for long-horizon interactive llm agents">2025</a>\],
MCTS-guided search \[Putta<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib63" class="ltx_ref"
title="Agent q: advanced reasoning and learning for autonomous ai agents">2024</a>\],
exploration-based trajectory
optimization \[Song<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib64" class="ltx_ref"
title="Trial and error: exploration-based trajectory optimization of llm agents">2024</a>\],
multi-turn self-evolution \[Wang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib85" class="ltx_ref"
title="Ragen: understanding self-evolution in llm agents via multi-turn reinforcement learning">2025e</a>,
Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib89" class="ltx_ref"
title="AgentRL: scaling agentic reinforcement learning with a multi-turn, multi-task framework">2025a</a>\],
and cross-episode meta-RL \[Jiang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib52" class="ltx_ref"
title="Meta-rl induces exploration in language agents">2025b</a>\].
Recent work further refines credit assignment via stepwise progress
attribution \[Wang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib87" class="ltx_ref"
title="SPA-rl: reinforcing llm agents via stepwise progress attribution">2025a</a>,
Wei<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib88" class="ltx_ref"
title="Reinforcing multi-turn reasoning in llm agents via turn-level reward design">2025a</a>\]
or intrinsic exploration
signals \[Gao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib90" class="ltx_ref"
title="Navigate the unknown: enhancing llm reasoning with intrinsic motivation guided exploration">2025</a>,
Wang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib91" class="ltx_ref"
title="Harnessing uncertainty: entropy-modulated policy gradients for long-horizon llm agents">2025b</a>\].
Prompt-based methods such as
ReAct \[Yao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib37" class="ltx_ref"
title="React: synergizing reasoning and acting in language models">2022b</a>\]
and Reflexion \[Shinn<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib38" class="ltx_ref"
title="Reflexion: language agents with verbal reinforcement learning">2023</a>\]
enable reasoning without parameter updates but are upper-bounded by the
frozen policy \[Abel<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib79" class="ltx_ref"
title="A definition of continual reinforcement learning">2023</a>\].
Skill1 extends GRPO by decomposing a single task-outcome signal into
stage-specific gradients for selection, utilization, and distillation
within a unified RL objective.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Experience Reusing.

<div id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Structuring past experience for reuse improves RL sample
efficiency \[Zhan<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref"
title="Exgrpo: learning to reason from experience">2025</a>,
Ye<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref"
title="Online experiential learning for language models">2026</a>,
Muhtar<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref"
title="Complementary reinforcement learning">2026</a>\], and explicit
memory systems that store interaction
histories \[Wei<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib23" class="ltx_ref"
title="Evo-memory: benchmarking llm agent test-time learning with self-evolving memory">2025b</a>,
Liu<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib51" class="ltx_ref"
title="SimpleMem: efficient lifelong memory for llm agents">2026a</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib92" class="ltx_ref"
title="Exploratory memory-augmented llm agent via hybrid on- and off-policy optimization">b</a>\]
or distilled lessons \[Fang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib70" class="ltx_ref"
title="MemP: exploring agent procedural memory">2025</a>,
Zhou<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib69" class="ltx_ref"
title="Memento: fine-tuning llm agents without fine-tuning llms">2025</a>,
Wang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib93" class="ltx_ref"
title="Cogito, ergo ludo: an agent that learns to play by reasoning and planning">2025d</a>\]
support continuous adaptation.
RetroAgent \[Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref"
title="RetroAgent: from solving to evolving via retrospective dual intrinsic feedback">2026b</a>\]
combines intrinsic progress rewards with language-based lesson
extraction and a utility-aware selection
strategy \[Auer<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib76" class="ltx_ref"
title="Finite-time analysis of the multiarmed bandit problem">2002</a>\].
Critique-GRPO \[Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib94" class="ltx_ref"
title="Critique-grpo: advancing llm reasoning with natural language and numerical feedback">2025b</a>\]
integrates natural-language critiques with numerical rewards, and
RL-based
self-distillation \[Hübotter<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib95" class="ltx_ref"
title="Reinforcement learning via self-distillation">2026</a>\] refines
failed trajectories into policy updates. Retrospective self-correction
through natural-language
critiques \[Madaan<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib56" class="ltx_ref"
title="Self-refine: iterative refinement with self-feedback">2023</a>,
Yao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib53" class="ltx_ref"
title="Retroformer: retrospective large language agents with policy gradient optimization">2023</a>\]
further enables agents to learn from failures \[Liu and Van Der Schaar,
<a href="#bib.bib57" class="ltx_ref"
title="Position: truly self-improving agents require intrinsic metacognitive learning">2025</a>\].
Skill1 builds on these insights but derives all learning signals from a
single task-outcome signal, eliminating the need for separate intrinsic
reward design.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Skill Libraries for LLM Agents.

<div id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

A growing body of work equips LLM agents with persistent skill
libraries \[Jiang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib33" class="ltx_ref"
title="SoK: agentic skills–beyond tool use in llm agents">2026b</a>, Xu
and Yan, <a href="#bib.bib34" class="ltx_ref"
title="Agent skills for large language models: architecture, acquisition, security, and the path forward">2026</a>,
Li<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib66" class="ltx_ref"
title="Organizing, orchestrating, and benchmarking agent skills at ecosystem scale">2026a</a>,
Jiang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref"
title="XSkill: continual learning from experience and skills in multimodal agents">2026a</a>,
Anthropic, <a href="#bib.bib75" class="ltx_ref"
title="Introducing agent skills">2025</a>\]. For selection, approaches
include frozen embedding
selectors \[Xia<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref"
title="Skillrl: evolving agents via recursive skill-augmented reinforcement learning">2026</a>,
Muhtar<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref"
title="Complementary reinforcement learning">2026</a>\], heuristic
scoring \[Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref"
title="RetroAgent: from solving to evolving via retrospective dual intrinsic feedback">2026b</a>\],
learned routing \[Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib48" class="ltx_ref"
title="MemSkill: learning and evolving memory skills for self-evolving agents">2026a</a>,
Wang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib32" class="ltx_ref"
title="SkillOrchestra: learning to route agents via skill transfer">2026</a>\],
and policy log-probability
ranking \[Li<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib20" class="ltx_ref"
title="Arise: agent reasoning with intrinsic skill evolution in hierarchical reinforcement learning">2026b</a>,
Wu<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib49" class="ltx_ref"
title="Evolver: self-evolving llm agents through an experience-driven lifecycle">2025</a>\].
For utilization, RL-based methods condition the policy on selected
skills \[Xia<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref"
title="Skillrl: evolving agents via recursive skill-augmented reinforcement learning">2026</a>,
Muhtar<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref"
title="Complementary reinforcement learning">2026</a>,
Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref"
title="RetroAgent: from solving to evolving via retrospective dual intrinsic feedback">2026b</a>,
Li<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib20" class="ltx_ref"
title="Arise: agent reasoning with intrinsic skill evolution in hierarchical reinforcement learning">2026b</a>,
Wang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib45" class="ltx_ref"
title="Reinforcement learning for self-improving agent with skill library">2025c</a>\],
sometimes with hierarchical rewards to incentivize skill
use \[Li<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib20" class="ltx_ref"
title="Arise: agent reasoning with intrinsic skill evolution in hierarchical reinforcement learning">2026b</a>,
Muhtar<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref"
title="Complementary reinforcement learning">2026</a>\]. For
distillation, methods range from prompt-based
extraction \[Zhao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib39" class="ltx_ref"
title="Expel: llm agents are experiential learners">2024</a>\] and
training-free skill
versioning \[Yang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref"
title="AutoSkill: experience-driven lifelong learning via skill self-evolution">2026</a>\]
to teacher-driven generation \[Xia<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref"
title="Skillrl: evolving agents via recursive skill-augmented reinforcement learning">2026</a>\],
co-evolving extractors \[Muhtar<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref"
title="Complementary reinforcement learning">2026</a>\], and
self-reflection \[Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref"
title="RetroAgent: from solving to evolving via retrospective dual intrinsic feedback">2026b</a>,
Wang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib45" class="ltx_ref"
title="Reinforcement learning for self-improving agent with skill library">2025c</a>,
Wu<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib49" class="ltx_ref"
title="Evolver: self-evolving llm agents through an experience-driven lifecycle">2025</a>,
Qiao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib26" class="ltx_ref"
title="Memory intelligence agent">2026</a>\]. Existing methods have not
yet achieved RL-optimized status on all three stages simultaneously, and
those that optimize multiple stages use heterogeneous learning signals
without a unified objective.
Skill0 \[Lu<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib46" class="ltx_ref"
title="SKILL0: in-context agentic reinforcement learning for skill internalization">2026</a>\]
internalizes skills into model parameters with zero external skills;
Skill1 co-evolves all three stages through one policy model and a
unified task outcome signal.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2" class="section ltx_appendix">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix B </span>Algorithm Details

<div id="A2.p1" class="ltx_para">

We use Group Relative Policy Optimization
(GRPO) \[Shao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref"
title="Deepseekmath: pushing the limits of mathematical reasoning in open language models">2024</a>\]
as the optimization method, which eliminates the need for a separate
value network by computing advantages relative to a group of rollouts
sampled from the same task. For each task $`d`$, a group of $`G`$
rollouts $`\{\tau_{i}\}_{i=1}^{G}`$ is sampled from
$`\pi_{\theta_{\text{old}}}`$. The group-relative advantage for rollout
$`i`$ is:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\hat{A}_{i}=\frac{r(\tau_{i})-\operatorname{mean}(\{r(\tau_{1}),\ldots,r(\tau_{G})\})}{\operatorname{std}(\{r(\tau_{1}),\ldots,r(\tau_{G})\})}.
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(12)</span> |

Let
$`\rho_{t}^{(i)}(\theta)=\pi_{\theta}(a_{t}^{(i)}\mid s_{t}^{(i)})/\pi_{\theta_{\text{old}}}(a_{t}^{(i)}\mid s_{t}^{(i)})`$
denote the per-token importance ratio. The GRPO objective maximizes the
clipped surrogate:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\mathcal{J}_{\text{GRPO}}(\theta)=\frac{1}{G}\sum_{i=1}^{G}\frac{1}{|\tau_{i}|}\sum_{t=1}^{|\tau_{i}|}\min\!\bigl(\rho_{t}^{(i)}\hat{A}_{i},\;\operatorname{clip}(\rho_{t}^{(i)},1{-}\epsilon,1{+}\epsilon)\,\hat{A}_{i}\bigr)-\beta\,D_{\text{KL}}\!\bigl[\pi_{\theta}\|\pi_{\text{ref}}\bigr],
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(13)</span> |

where $`\epsilon`$ is the clipping ratio, $`\beta`$ controls KL
regularization toward a reference policy $`\pi_{\text{ref}}`$, and
$`|\tau_{i}|`$ is the number of tokens in rollout $`i`$.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3" class="section ltx_appendix">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix C </span>Implementation Details

<div id="A3.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Training infrastructure.

<div id="A3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Skill1 is trained on 8 NVIDIA H800-80GB GPUs using the VeRL
framework \[Sheng<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib81" class="ltx_ref"
title="HybridFlow: a flexible and efficient rlhf framework">2024</a>\]
with Fully Sharded Data Parallelism (FSDP) under BFloat16 precision.
Rollout generation uses vLLM with tensor parallelism of 4. Training
converges in approximately 100 to 150 steps (roughly 30 hours on
ALFWorld). The auxiliary objective weights are
$`\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=0.3`$ throughout all experiments unless
otherwise specified.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS0.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Baseline reproduction.

<div id="A3.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

We reproduce RetroAgent using its official
implementation.<span id="footnote1"
class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup>1</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup>1</sup><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">1</span><a href="https://github.com/zhangxy-2019/RetroAgent"
class="ltx_ref ltx_url ltx_font_typewriter"
target="_blank">https://github.com/zhangxy-2019/RetroAgent</a></span></span></span>
For SkillRL, EvolveR, Mem0, and SimpleMem, we use numbers reported in
their respective papers \[Xia<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref"
title="Skillrl: evolving agents via recursive skill-augmented reinforcement learning">2026</a>,
Wu<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib49" class="ltx_ref"
title="Evolver: self-evolving llm agents through an experience-driven lifecycle">2025</a>,
Chhikara<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref"
title="Mem0: building production-ready ai agents with scalable long-term memory">2025</a>,
Liu<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib51" class="ltx_ref"
title="SimpleMem: efficient lifelong memory for llm agents">2026a</a>\]
under the same base model (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct). GiGPO results are taken
from Feng<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
\[<a href="#bib.bib14" class="ltx_ref"
title="Group-in-group policy optimization for llm agent training">2025</a>\].
All RL baselines use identical training budgets (150 epochs) and the
same train/test splits to ensure fair comparison.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS0.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Hyperparameters.

<div id="A3.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a> lists the shared training
hyperparameters across both environments.
Table <a href="#A3.T5" class="ltx_ref"

class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a> lists the per-environment
differences. Table <a href="#A3.T6" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 6 ‣ Hyperparameters. ‣ Appendix C Implementation Details ‣ Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a> lists the skill library
configuration.

</div>
